# THE PRESAGES, PRO-
### *phecies, and Divina- tions of*
# AUREOLUS THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS
<note>Aureolus Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a revolutionary Swiss physician, alchemist, and philosopher. He is often credited as the "father of toxicology" and was a pivotal figure in the shift toward chemical medicine in the Renaissance.</note>

---

### ALSO ASTRONOMICAL and Astrological Fragments, pleasant and useful to read.
<note>In this era, "astronomical" and "astrological" were often used interchangeably. For Paracelsus, the movements of the stars were not just celestial events but were linked to the "inner stars" or vital forces within the human body and the earth's minerals.</note>

# BASEL,
### *AT THE HOUSE OF PETER PERNA, 1569.*
<note>original: "BASILEAE, APVD PETRVM PERnam." Basel was a major European center for intellectual life and printing. Peter Perna was a renowned printer who specialized in the works of Paracelsus and other occult and medical authors.</note>

<summary>This page is the title page for a 1569 edition of Paracelsus's writings on prophecy, divination, and the celestial sciences. It establishes the work as a collection of both visionary predictions and practical astronomical fragments published in the intellectual hub of Basel.</summary>

The translator addresses Charles II, Margrave of Baden-Durlach (1529–1577), a significant ruler who introduced the Reformation to his territories and was a known patron of learning.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS
## PRINCE CHARLES, MAR-
## GRAVE OF BADEN AND HACH-
### berg, Landgrave of Sausenberg, Lord
### of Rötteln, Badenweiler,
### etc. Greetings.

Since I have translated certain fragments of the *Great Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophiae magnae." This refers to Paracelsus's cosmological and metaphysical teachings.</note> of Theophrastus Paracelsus from the German tongue into Latin, so that they might be understood even by foreign nations, and since I have dedicated them to your Most Illustrious Highness—the sequence of the work itself demonstrates that as many books of this philosophy are still required as there have been chapters published thus far—and when those books which have been held in "prisons" <note>The translator uses the metaphor of "prisons" (carceribus) to describe unpublished manuscripts held in private collections or libraries, hidden from the public eye.</note> for many years finally emerge into the light, God willing, we shall endeavor to ensure that they too may become known to foreigners under your name:

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of a Latin translation of Paracelsus's works to Charles, Margrave of Baden, explaining the translator's goal of making German philosophy accessible to a wider European audience.</summary>
<keywords>Charles II Margrave of Baden, dedication, Paracelsus, Latin translation, Great Philosophy, Renaissance patronage, Sausenberg, Rötteln</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Charles II, Margrave of Baden. The translator, likely Adam von Bodenstein or a similar contemporary editor of Paracelsus, explains that while many of Paracelsus's practical works are still missing or suppressed, he is publishing these "theoretical" fragments to preserve the author's legacy and share German wisdom with the rest of Europe.</meta>

# PREFACE.

Finally, other certain **Astronomical** and **Astrological** works by the same author have come to light. These contain only the **Theory** of the seven practical books—which are also much desired—of the complete art. This art has been mutilated until now and restricted to a single form, even though true **Astronomy** <note>In the 16th century, "Astronomy" and "Astrology" were often used interchangeably or as two sides of the same study of the heavens.</note> contains five highly necessary parts.

Therefore, satisfied with these crumbs <note>original: "micis." The translator uses this humble term to describe the fragmentary nature of the surviving manuscripts.</note>, until we may possess the complete books of our teacher—which are currently held back by envy <note>original: "invidia." This refers to the professional jealousy or censorship that prevented Paracelsus's work from being published during his lifetime.</note>—we strive with all our strength, as is proper for good students, to rescue our teacher’s name and the glory of **German** scholarship from obscurity. 

Furthermore, we wish to make those foreigners who thirst for the praiseworthy and true arts we enjoy—such as the **Lower Germans**, the **French**, the **Italians**, and others—partakers of such great learning and wisdom. For we are not born for ourselves alone, but also for our neighbors <note>This is a reference to a famous Stoic sentiment, popularized by Cicero, that humans have a social duty to contribute to the common good.</note>. Therefore, our efforts

<summary>The translator describes his effort to recover and publish Paracelsus's fragmented astronomical works, aiming to share German intellectual achievements with the rest of Europe (France, Italy, and the Low Countries).</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, astrology, Paracelsus, German scholarship, manuscript preservation, European intellectual exchange, Cicero, social duty</keywords>

# PREFACE
a 3

...our [works], Most Illustrious Prince; since they are honest and pious, I pray that you deign to help and also promote them with your favor and patronage, as you are well able to do. Thus it shall be that, safe from the malice <note>original: "livore," meaning professional envy or spite</note> and the biting tongue <note>original: "dente," literally "tooth," a common Renaissance metaphor for harsh criticism</note> of those who strive to resist the truth, we may be able to complete this work which is so highly necessary for the Republic of Letters <term>Republic of Letters: The international community of scholars and intellectuals who exchanged ideas across borders</term> and the common good. Farewell, Most Serene Prince, and live happily.

->Humble Servant,<-

# P. THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS ON PRESAGES, PROPHECIES, AND DIVINATIONS.

He wrote astronomical and astrological works, from which these theological <note>original: "theoica"; Paracelsus often used this term to describe a form of divine or spiritual philosophy distinct from academic theology.</note> fragments were selected by several people.

->From the book on the art of foreknowledge<-

Of himself, man knows nothing of the future, nor was he even created to know or investigate such things; rather, he was made for the knowledge of himself—namely, to lift his soul toward God. For each day has its own care. However, this should not be understood regarding the day of Christ, but of men, for whom death is to be expected today, and does not only come tomorrow. Likewise, in other bodily matters, care should be taken for them today, not tomorrow: this alone befits us, to pray to God constantly, to seek His Kingdom above all things, etc. Why is it that we turn our minds toward the presages of the future (which...

<summary>This opening page of Paracelsus's treatise argues that humans are not naturally meant to know the future, emphasizing instead self-knowledge and daily devotion to God. It establishes a theological boundary for the "art of foreknowledge," suggesting that spiritual readiness for death is more vital than predicting future events.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, divination, prophecy, self-knowledge, theology, astrology, providence, memento mori</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening argument of the treatise. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the nature of future presages.</meta>

### THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS

...are various) since they delight the body and seem to be useful only to it, but not to the soul. Those who do not seek the Kingdom of God are concerned only with themselves, giving no care to God. The result is that they become greedy for wonders, always preoccupied with investigating and testing first this thing and then that. For this reason, God grants them nothing; He permits them to act only according to their own worldly wisdom, whether it turns out well or poorly.

<margin>Prophets</margin>
The reason I am writing a book about these things is that the prophecies of the prophets and the Sibyls <note>The Sibyls were ancient prophetesses of the Greek and Roman world whose oracles were often interpreted by later Christian writers as foreshadowing Christ.</note>, as well as natural and supernatural signs that occur and are presented to us daily, have origins that must be sought among the Gentiles <note>original: "Gentilibus"; referring here to the non-Christian or pagan nations of antiquity.</note>. If these people had fully committed themselves to God, they would have been free from such cares and anxieties. However, such omens are permitted by God for the warning of wicked men toward repentance <term>repentance: the act of turning away from sin and toward God</term>, and for no other reason. And although such signs appear one after another over a long period of time, and so far apart that they seem by their nature to be beneath notice, they nonetheless arise from the same mud <note>original: "limo"; Paracelsus likely uses this to mean they share the same base or fundamental origin.</note>. For this reason, I now desire to set such things forth, with this exception, namely
->when<-

<summary>Paracelsus argues that while worldly wisdom and curiosity about the future are distractions from God, divine omens and ancient prophecies are permitted by God specifically to drive sinners toward repentance. He acknowledges that while many signs have pagan origins, they still stem from a singular source and serve a divine purpose.</summary>

<keywords>worldly wisdom, Kingdom of God, prophecy, Sibyls, Gentiles, omens, repentance, providence</keywords>

### ON PRESAGES
a 5

when God's mercy and His Kingdom have been sought, we are delivered from storms, from war, high prices <note>original: "caris annonis"; literally "dear harvests," referring to periods of scarcity or famine where grain prices soared.</note>, and so forth, for we then exist under the protection of God, who commands and conquers all things. The prognostications of diseases—since these require physicians—have a solid foundation; yet common presages <note>original: "præsagia"; omens or signs of the future.</note> often have nothing upon which to lean. Nevertheless, the oracles of the prophets and the Sibyls <note>Ancient prophetesses believed by many Renaissance Christians to have predicted the coming of Christ and other divine truths.</note> do have their own foundation: since they are created by God, they do not come from us, but from the justice of God alone. There exist five species of prophecies in general: the first, that of the 

<margin>The five species of prophecy.</margin>

prophets and the Sibyls, is the most firmly established, because it arises from God alone and not from any craft or good fortune; for this reason, they say and do nothing of themselves. The second is natural, when nature itself indicates its own coming troubles to us through its own proper signs, according to the end—that is, the purpose toward which it tends. The third species is Divination <term>divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future through supernatural or intellectual means</term>, which is performed by men through the meditation of their own "inner light" <note>original: "proprii luminis"; likely referring to the Paracelsian "Light of Nature," the innate human capacity to understand the world.</note>, by which one can discover a judgment concerning a matter and know where it leads. The fourth is sorcery <note>original: "sortilegium"; literally "the casting of lots," but often used for various forms of folk magic or divination.</note> and the presage of spirits, according to the condition by which a person conceives an imagination with them, or

<meta>catchword: as [ut]</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus distinguishes between reliable divine prophecy and other forms of foretelling, listing the first four of five categories of prophecy including natural signs and human divination.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, Sibyls, divination, Light of Nature, sorcery, famine, divine protection</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the five species of prophecy. The previous page ended with a description of the third species (divination) and began discussing the fourth or fifth, leading into the truth that favors humanity.</meta>

->THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS<-

ng the truth, so that they themselves favor men in truth. <note>original: "vt ipsi fauent hominibus ex veritate"; this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding how divine or natural forces act in accordance with truth for human benefit.</note>

The fifth is called **augury** <term>augury: the practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of animals or natural phenomena</term>, which is received from animals and creatures. From these, one may know what might happen to men, such as the flooding of rivers or waters, the onslaught of storms and winds, and so forth. 

**Prophets**, **Sibyls**, and **Apostles** are all of the same rank, for they proceed from the one mouth of God. They possess two 

<margin>True and divine prophets</margin>

offices or duties: the first is for consolation, and the second is for punishment. The consolation of the prophets and the Sibyls <note>Prophetesses from Greek and Roman mythology whom many Renaissance scholars believed had also foretold the coming of Christ.</note> is the announcement of the **Messiah**. These prophecies must proceed from God the Father, for it is necessary for like to come from like. 

Likewise, the prophecies of the **Last Judgment** <note>original: "vltimi iudicij"; the final divine assessment of humanity at the end of time.</note> are also for the consolation of the faithful, and they must be received from the prophets, Christ, and the Apostles. For punishment, there is a twofold office: the first is in this life, as seen in the devastation and changes of kingdoms, and so forth; the second is in the Last Judgment. All these things indeed proceed from God and Scripture—from God, when He Himself permits them from among His own. 

All judgments that are made from signs concerning the Last Judgment are false; for the less men have suspected it, and the more secure they have felt from it, [the more suddenly it will come]. <note>The text here mirrors the biblical warning that the end will come "like a thief in the night."</note>

<summary>Paracelsus defines augury as a natural form of prophecy and elevates Prophets, Sibyls, and Apostles as divine messengers who provide both comfort and warnings of judgment. He argues that while natural signs can predict storms or political ruin, the timing of the Last Judgment remains hidden from all human calculation.</summary>

<keywords>augury, prophecy, Sibyls, Messiah, Last Judgment, divine punishment, consolation, natural signs</keywords>

### ON FOREWARNINGS
<note>original: "DE PRAESAGI."; derived from the Latin *praesagium*, referring to a sign or omen of a future event.</note>

...will come. In this life, upheavals <note>original: "eversiones"; can refer to revolutions, overthrows, or major social collapses.</note> will still occur just as in the Old Testament, but their timing is known to God alone. For this reason, those who speak of such things in simplicity and do not themselves understand what they say are to be most greatly observed, because they speak from the mouth of God: a thing which is impossible to achieve through any human art. Of these people, three types exist: the Prophets, the Sibyls, and the Apostles of Christ. For these are simple by condition—not ambitious, nor greedy, etc. From this, it follows that they were despised by the wise of the world; because while those "wise" ones see and hear, they do not truly see or hear; so also God wills it to be.

Whatever forewarnings are not of this first kind <note>i.e., divine revelation through simple people.</note> are to be considered sectarian <note>original: "sectaria"; here meaning secondary, factional, or originating from a specific school of thought rather than universal divine truth.</note>, as if originating from created things. Yet, those which speak from nature are greater than those which are received through sorcery <note>original: "fortilegiis"; specifically the practice of foretelling the future by drawing lots or other superstitious means.</note>.

<margin>The foreknowledge of demons.</margin>
To the spirits existing in the elements <note>Paracelsus refers here to "elementals"—beings like Gnomes or Undines that he believed inhabited the natural world.</note>, all things are known which are possible to be known through nature, whether they be prosperous or unhappy for regions and peoples. For example, when an infant is born, they know from the beginning to the very end of its life what might happen to it, and they know the seasons of the years from the beginning to the end, day by day. Likewise in war, they know the victory and...

<summary>Paracelsus distinguishes between divine prophecy—delivered through simple, unlearned people like Sibyls and Apostles—and the natural foreknowledge possessed by elemental spirits.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, Sibyls, elemental spirits, foreknowledge, divine revelation, sorcery, Paracelsus</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "elemental spirits" from page 5. While the previous page ended with the spirits' capacity to know future events, this page clarifies the limitations God has placed upon their ability to communicate that knowledge to humanity, shifting then to a discussion of natural, astronomical cycles.</meta>

->### THEOPHR. PARA.<-
<note>Short for Theophrastus Paracelsus, the pen name of the Swiss physician and alchemist Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541).</note>

...succumbing, etc. In short, whatever man and nature do, these spirits know before it happens, right up to the end. But because they have been made mute and deceitful <note>original: "mendaces"; Paracelsus often argued that while elemental spirits were not inherently evil like devils, they were untrustworthy and prone to trickery.</note>, they are not able to indicate things to men as clearly or plainly as they themselves know them. For God does not permit it; yet, as much as they are able, they favor men and offer signs. <margin>Deceitful spirits.</margin> Therefore, arts of this kind are most deceptive; though some truth is contained in them, there is nothing grounded or certain to be found. This is the degree of faith to be placed in spirits: no more than the extent to which they happen to hit the mark, which rarely occurs, for everything of theirs is full of doubt.

<margin>Natural signs.</margin>
Naturally, these things happen in this way: The Heavens follow a course like a traveler on earth, which can only be known through long experience, based on what the Heavens work in this or that place, or at this or that time. According to the Moon, the course runs from one influence to another; its path is terminated in one way here, and in another way elsewhere. Just as an experienced and steady mule will not pass a blacksmith’s shop until its horseshoes have been struck with a hammer <note>A rustic analogy suggesting that natural cycles are as predictable as the habits of a working animal.</note>, the same can be judged regarding the stars and the seasons of the year—whatever happens in summer or winter...

<vocab>Theophrastus Paracelsus, Demons, spirits, Heaven, Moon, nature</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus warns that while elemental spirits possess foreknowledge, they are restricted by God and inherently untrustworthy, making spiritual arts unreliable. He contrasts this with "natural" foreknowledge, which is based on the predictable, rhythmic cycles of the Heavens and the Moon.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, elemental spirits, divination, deceit, astrology, lunar cycles, natural philosophy, blacksmith analogy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 6 regarding natural signs and the predictability of celestial movements. While the previous page used the analogy of a mule and a blacksmith to describe rhythmic cycles, this page expands the metaphor to a traveler to explain how knowledge of "concordance" (the harmony between different parts of nature) allows for the prediction of future events.</meta>

# ON FORECASTS <note>Latin: "DE PRAESAGI." Paracelsus uses "presages" to refer to natural predictions based on observing the patterns of the physical and celestial worlds.</note>

...what each thing works. Since these things are therefore established with certainty, concordance <note>The doctrine of correspondence, which suggests that the "microcosm" (man) and "macrocosm" (the universe) reflect one another.</note> teaches which events <note>Latin: "accidentia." In this context, it refers to specific occurrences, properties, or "incidents" rather than mistakes.</note> are to come at this or that time. This is no different than among those traveling on earth toward those places in which various incidents are known, and others too, until <margin>The Traveler.</margin> one has arrived at them. For example, the incidents of walking or riding arise either from oneself or from an object: from oneself, such as becoming weary or turning lame; from an object, such as having to cross waters or mountains. Likewise, one might stay at an inn or hear something new, etc. From such things, forecasts are drawn; it is judged that this or that will happen because the beginnings of them have already been cast.

The Heavens likewise must be understood according to their own nature, along which they proceed; he who has learned this will also be able to point out the celestial travelers <note>original: "coelestes... peregrinos." This likely refers to the planets, which were traditionally called "wandering stars."</note> to men. Likewise, many and various houses <note>The twelve divisions of the sky used in astrology to determine influences on human life.</note> exist in the sky, none similar to another, with diverse daily works and wondrous journeys. Indeed, whoever understands all these things knows how to predict many natural occurrences throughout the years. <margin>The human condition is twofold, and of the soul.</margin>

Some place the foundation of these things upon the accidents of the *limbus* <term>Limbus: In Paracelsian philosophy, the "Great Limbus" is the primordial material or "matrix" of the universe from which the physical body of man was created.</term>, that is, from the ascending signs in this manner: Man has two conditions, one from the *limbus*, the other from the soul...

<vocab>presages, concordance, journey, heaven, limbus, soul, accidents</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that predicting the future is possible through "concordance," comparing the movements of the stars to a traveler whose path and challenges can be anticipated. He also introduces the "Limbus" as a fundamental material state of human existence that influences these natural signs.</summary>

<keywords>astronomy, Paracelsus, Limbus, presages, concordance, celestial houses, human condition, astrology</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 7 regarding how the heavens influence human life. While the previous page used the metaphor of a traveler encountering obstacles on a road to explain how one can forecast the future, this page moves into the specific application of astrology—how the positions of the stars at the moment of birth (the "erection" of a horoscope) dictate a person's path, and how education can potentially redirect that natural "instinct."</meta>

-># THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS<-

...nature, however, from the soul. When, therefore, at this or that minute, the figure <note>original: "figura... erecta." This refers to the "erection" or casting of an astrological chart (horoscope) at the moment of birth.</note> of anyone born in any place has been established, in which these heavens are constituted in this or that manner—that is to say, the **celestial men** <note>original: "coelestes homines." Paracelsus often spoke of "celestial" or "astral" counterparts to earthly humans, representing the spiritual or archetypal influences of the stars.</note> are present in that place, <margin>Birth charts</margin> namely in the **firmament** <term>firmament: the sphere of the fixed stars and planets</term>, they take up the governance of the newborn who is ignorant of all things on earth, and according to their own condition, they lead him wherever they wish. He who already knows these kinds of travelers and messengers, where they strike and what their conditions are, also knows how to liken the newborn to them. Thus far it is proper to speak of the firmament, and no further; and there is such a comparison to be made on earth: just as if two <margin>The double path of the newborn</margin> or three people wished to travel somewhere, and at that same moment another departed, unaware of them; if they were to persuade this person to be a companion of their journey and he agreed, he would <margin>Education and training alter nature.</margin> now be in their society, sharing their condition and their circumstances. This is one path.

The other path, however, is when this newborn is otherwise educated and instructed by parents or a teacher; then that **natural instinct** is twisted in another direction. It rarely happens that infants are educated or taught according to their own nature; from this arise the erections of nativities <note>The practice of drawing up a birth chart to determine one's fate or character.</note> in which...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that the stars (the "celestial men") govern a person's life from the moment of birth, but notes that education and upbringing can steer a person away from this original "natural instinct."</summary>

# ON FOREWARNINGS

...societies, <note>original: "societatibus." This completes the word "soc-" from the previous page's catchword.</note> in which not only natural travels <note>original: "peregrinationes." Paracelsus uses this to mean both physical journeys and the metaphorical path of a person's life.</note> but also the guidance of men—such as that of parents, teachers, and others—are included. From these, successes and misfortunes are usually judged. However, this art is not a natural one, but rather depends upon spirits who reveal such things in the following way.

First, the Astrologer <term>Astrologus: in the 16th century, this term referred to someone who studied both astronomy and the influence of the stars on human health and fate.</term> uses the judgment of natural travel derived from the stars to see with what prosperity or misfortune they might lead the newborn, whether toward poverty or toward riches. Here follows the next point: alongside these, spirits exist in the heavens who mingle themselves with the stars and accompany the newborn together with those stars. 

Just as a person also has a favorable spirit with them, these spirits strive to perform the duty of a traveler alongside the natural influences, but in another way: namely, so that they might lead the newborn through a path other than that of the stars—that is, according to their own will. For they are not idle; they hardly rest with us for a moment, from the beginning of birth throughout the rest of life. 

Therefore, one must observe with the greatest diligence this kind of spirit mixed into the **ascendant** <term>ascendant: the specific degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth, believed to govern the individual's personality and physical life.</term>, because it will be able to accomplish greater things than the ascendant itself or the stars. Furthermore, in these spirits, every kind of evil and vice...

<margin>False Astrologers.</margin>

Has not the devil inserted himself into Astronomy and Astrology so artfully that he passes himself off in place of the stars, so that judgments may be founded upon him? He has known how to hide himself so cautiously that, until now, he has been held in the highest regard; he has secretly inspired his own ways into the wise men of the world. Nor did they think otherwise until this point, but instead attributed these things to the heavens, its houses <term>houses: twelve divisions of the celestial sphere in astrology, each representing a different area of life (e.g., wealth, marriage, career).</term>, aspects, conjunctions, and so forth. ### ON FOREKNOWLEDGE
<note>original: "DE PRAESAGI"; likely an abbreviation for De Praesagiis, meaning "On Foreknowledge" or "On Omens."</note>

...these spirits <note>original: "ritus isti"; in this context, it refers to the spiritual influences or "rites" discussed previously.</note> inspired these things in them, so that despite everything, they might desire to lead the newborn in their own way, wherever they wish. He always keeps his own followers as an ape <note>original: "vt simia"; a common medieval and Renaissance metaphor for the devil imitating God’s creations in a distorted or mockery-filled way.</note> mimics its master—having false prophets, and likewise false astronomers; similarly, in the study of the elements, there are false physicists <term>physicists: "physicos," here referring to natural philosophers or physicians who study the physical laws of nature.</term>, and so forth. 

Therefore, if we did not meddle in these affairs, the devil would have no place among us. But because we long to know things that nature cannot reveal, the devil offers his assistance to us, even when we are unaware. For if he can openly possess a man, he can surely do so in secret. Therefore, cease these arts, you wicked physicists and pharisees, and cling instead to the word of God.

# THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS
<note>The header refers to the author, Theophrastus von Hohenheim, commonly known as Paracelsus.</note>

...by the inspiration of spiritual rites, which stirred the first inventors of these arts by possessing them <note>original: "obsidendo"; in the context of Renaissance demonology, "obsession" referred to a spirit influencing a person from the outside, whereas "possession" was internal.</note>: these men afterwards devised such arts out of that obsession; and finally, disciples learned them from those inventors. Some have attributed these arts to God, yet God in no way desires us to be skilled in such matters; rather, He wishes that we learn His precepts and live according to them. Although they receive their spirits from God and not from the Devil, it is nonetheless forbidden for them to teach us. They are not permitted to do so, and for this reason God punishes them; thus, they do not reveal the perfect truth, but rather many things that are highly doubtful and false—that is, partly true, but for the greater part false. They do this because they are disobedient to God, and so that they may seduce men into idolatry and compel them to act against God’s commandment, which forbids us from being anxious about tomorrow, and so forth. <note>A reference to the biblical command in Matthew 6:34: "Therefore do not worry about tomorrow, for tomorrow will worry about itself."</note>

<margin>Geomancy.</margin>

It consists of sixteen figures or signs which, when arranged according to their condition and nature—as if such a thing existed within nature itself—[govern] the translations, marks <note>original: "cretam," literally "chalk," referring to the medium used to draw the points.</note>, forms, points, and the entire figure according to its own rules...

# THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS
<note>The header refers to the author, Theophrastus von Hohenheim, commonly known as Paracelsus.</note>

...teaches how to cover. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing a thought from the previous page regarding the rules of the art.</note> Spirits lead the hand until as many points are noted as are required to form a judgment on the matter being considered. For the spirits know how many points are needed so that a figure <term>Figure: In geomancy, a 'figure' is a specific pattern of dots—such as 'The Way' or 'The People'—used to interpret an answer.</term> may result which demonstrates the very thing being sought; therefore, they lead the hand and guide it correctly. 

For example, I might desire to know what color garment a person <margin>*A geomantic example*</margin> standing outside the doors is wearing, whom I have not seen. To this end, the spirit proposes seven colors and assigns a geomantic sign to each; it then teaches how to mark out the figure. The spirit itself knows the color, while I do not understand the matter myself; yet it leads and guides my hand so that I include a certain number of points in a line, deleting them until the correct color is indicated; and such it truly is. 

Spirits, who know all things, work in no other way in all their figures, which is why they easily set them down: for the whole matter consists in the direction of the hands. At length, they have attached superstition to this art so that they might trouble men further, teaching that it should be done at night or in the bright sun. Likewise, they teach that one should pray first and say nothing afterward; yet this prayer <note>original: "orratio"; Paracelsus suggests that the ritualistic incantations or "orations" added to the practice do not improve the actual mechanism of the art.</note> is no better than the art itself. There is a twofold "ascendant" <note>original: "ascendens"; in astrology and divination, this refers to the rising influence or determining factor.</note> in this place: namely, the stars and the spirits.

<summary>Paracelsus explains that geomancy works through spirits physically guiding a practitioner's hand to create patterns of dots. He argues that while the mechanism is real, spirits often add unnecessary superstitious rituals like specific timings or prayers to further confuse and ensnare humanity.</summary>
<keywords>geomancy, spirits, divination, superstition, astrology, ritual prayer, hidden knowledge</keywords>

-># ON PREDICTIONS<-

...concerns Astronomy, and the spirit concerns Pyromancy <term>Pyromancy: Divination by means of fire or the appearance of flames.</term>, from which a birth chart is cast, as well as from names. In Pyromancy, the spirit clings to the star; when the Pyromancer reveals the star's condition, he reveals the devil and his condition through concordances, conjunctions, squares <note>original: "quadrata"; astrological aspects where planets are 90 degrees apart, often considered stressful or challenging.</note>, and so on. From this, one thing or another follows because the devil is at work within them. 

<margin>
*The rising stars stand for Astronomy.*
*The rising spirit stands for Pyromancy.*
</margin>

He now attributes this to the stars and the elements, and thus establishes a double idolatry: more is attributed to the stars and the devil hidden beneath them than to God. Thus, men become liars alongside the devil himself, wasting their time on these vain fantasies. For they lie so remarkably that if, by some stroke of luck, one truth should emerge out of twenty divinations <note>original: "sortilegiis"; referring here to the casting of lots or general fortune-telling.</note>, the practitioner tries to make the nineteen lies seem true, so that the spirit may trouble these men all the more. 

Because these practitioners are compelled to lie by their very nature, the masters of fire <term>Pyronomi: A term derived from Greek (pyr, 'fire' and nomos, 'law'), referring to those who study the laws or patterns of fire-divination.</term> cannot find a true foundation. At one moment this is missing, at another the "exaltation" <note>An astrological term for the position of a planet where it is thought to exert its most powerful or beneficial influence.</note> is lacking. In this way, they waste their time; even after several thousand years, this art has still not been truly discovered or perfected, for its foundation is nothing other than lying. Therefore, where the devil...

<summary>Paracelsus critiques astronomy and pyromancy as practiced through spirits, arguing that they lead to a "double idolatry" where the devil is prioritized over God. He concludes that these arts are fundamentally built on deception and have failed to find a true basis despite millennia of practice.</summary>

-># ON PREDICTIONS<-

<center>b 3</center>

...the [devil] <note>original: "diabolus"; the subject is carried over from the previous page's discussion on the spirit's influence.</note> wishes to work his will, he suggests it to the pyromancer, who then declares that in such a conjunction a house will be consumed by fire. This then follows, because the devil himself sets it ablaze; thus, this one thing comes to pass, while the other nineteen do not occur. All arts of this kind, and those that follow, were for the most part first devised by the Greeks. This nation of men was, above all others, naturally inclined and suited to every kind of lie, and was likewise obedient and subject to spirits.

<margin>Lying Greeks.</margin>
Geomancy <term>Geomancy: Divination using earth, such as throwing soil or interpreting marks made in the ground.</term>, Pyromancy <term>Pyromancy: Divination by observing fire and flames.</term>, and so on, derive their names from the elements, and rightly so; for every element has its own stars and its own heaven, though spirits mingle themselves into all of them. Therefore, these four arts were organized through these spirits, who infused their influence into the stars within those elements.

<margin>Hydromancy.</margin>
Hydromancy <term>Hydromancy: Divination by means of water, including its movements, colors, or images seen within it.</term> is likewise established in figures and signs, together with celestial patterns, in this manner: Having looked into a vessel full of water, or gazing into it, observe the direction in which the waves flow, their trembling and their stillness, and likewise the bubbles. These four characteristics produce four figures, and each of those produces four others, making sixteen in total, from which the rules of the art are derived.

<summary>This page explains how the devil manipulates fire-divination to make occasional "true" predictions and attributes the invention of these deceptive arts to the Greeks. It also describes the elemental basis of geomancy and pyromancy, and details the specific method for practicing hydromancy using water patterns.</summary>

# ON PREDICTIONS

...[produce] <note>The text completes a word from the previous page, likely 'bullæ' (bubbles) or a verb related to producing signs.</note> so that one figure signifies this thing, and another signifies that, and so on. The spirit effects these things through waves, trembling, stillness, and the like, according to the requirements of the sign, so that a figure answering the question results from it.

Truly, he who has favoring spirits, and who trusts not in God nor wishes to rely [on Him], can turn out to be a successful diviner <note>original: "sortilegum"; a practitioner who predicts the future through omens, lots, or signs.</note>. On the other hand, he who has a false and envious spirit attached to himself will turn out poorly. For a spirit favors one person more than another; for this reason, one diviner is also more of a liar than another. 

Therefore, whenever one diviner proves better than another, it is not achieved by skill, but by the conditions of the spirits. This is a satanic trick: to engineer enmities and hatreds among his own practitioners, so that wretched mortals are sufficiently tormented by him, and do not see that the art—over which their hatred is directed—is entirely frivolous and empty, and possesses nothing of certainty. 

<margin>Divination is uncertain.</margin>
For example, if I consult a diviner about whether I should take a certain woman as a wife or not, and he tells me she will not be mine and that I shall take another, one must then wait for the outcome of this matter from both sides. It is not, therefore, a certain science through which the devil will work or not.

Necromancy <term>Necromancy: Here, Paracelsus likely refers not just to conjuring the dead, but to a broader category of "sidereal" or spirit-led divination.</term> or Chaomancy <term>Chaomancy: Divination by means of the "chaos" or the air, such as interpreting visions, shapes, or aerial phenomena.</term> pertains to the air...

-># OF PROPHECY<-
->b 4<-

[It] is an art and a science. Although Necromancy <term>Necromancy: Traditionally defined as communication with the dead, the author here shifts the focus to the "science of shades" or apparitions found in the element of air.</term> is defined in other ways elsewhere, its true definition is the "science of shades." This means that in the air, nothing else is found except for these shades alone, through which matters of this kind are understood. <margin>Necromancy or Chaomancy <term>Chaomancy: A form of divination using the "chaos" of the air, such as cloud formations or atmospheric visions.</term></margin> These arts are various and manifold, yet they are visible to humans so that it can be perceived by their eyes what things exist within them. For instance, some people see figures in the air at night, just as when such figures sometimes appear in the heavens as omens, prefiguring some event.

Sometimes men are even seen wandering in the air, and they are heard to make the sound of clashing weapons, and a great tumult arises. Occasionally shades are seen in water; similarly, they are observed in crystals, beryls <note>original: "beryllis"; a transparent gemstone, like an emerald or aquamarine, used in scrying.</note>, mirrors, thumbnails <note>original: "unguibus pollicum"; this refers to onychomancy, an ancient practice of coating a fingernail in oil or wax to observe reflections or visions.</note>, and so forth. The foundation of all these things is situated in spirits, according to whether they wish to manifest themselves or not, whether in this person or in another.

Whatever men desire to learn from these [spirits], the spirits teach them, but always with the addition of some superstition. For example, the spirits may require men to pray and conjure them by force to make them act, as if they would not have done so otherwise. Thus, wretched mortals think they can compel spirits through the power of God, yet God does not wish for this at all. Here, [spirits] most effectively lead astray... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the author is likely warning that spirits use the "requirement" of ritual or prayer to trick the practitioner into believing they have control.</note>

-># ON OMENS<-
->## b 5<-

...[it] knows, and flies toward it; so also the peacock has a nature for perceiving a future corpse by scent: many similar things are found. Crows likewise have an innate condition for announcing a plague <note>original: "pestem"</note> not yet revealed by their croaking. Whoever has learned to understand such croakings of birds will be able to predict the plague, pleurisy <term>Pleurisy: An inflammation of the lining of the lungs, often used historically to describe various sharp chest pains or respiratory infections.</term>, and other future illnesses. These things are natural: for birds possess by nature the ability to often know those things which the man whom they concern knows least of all, because the five senses of beasts far surpass the senses of humans. 

<margin>
The excellence of the five senses: the owl in sight, the vulture in smell, the shrew <note>original: "sorex"</note> in hearing, the ape in touch, the parrot in taste.
</margin>

For example, the owl surpasses in sight, the vulture in smell, and the shrew in hearing. In touch, the ape perceives all the pulses of the veins better than a man; for when it touches death or a corrupt disease in any limb, it indicates this very thing by turning away its nose in horror. Parrots surpass any other animals in taste by choosing those things which are most healthy, according to what is best for the bird in its season. From these five senses of these animals, a judgment can be made according to each one's natural property and through experience (which is most agreeable to nature); and from these things, more can be learned more broadly than is possible for any...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that animals possess sensory perceptions far superior to humans, allowing them to detect illness and death before they manifest. He categorizes these animal omens as natural phenomena rather than spiritual deceptions.</summary>

<keywords>natural omens, animal senses, plague prediction, pleurisy, vulture, owl, shrew, ape, parrot, medical diagnosis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the heightened senses of animals from page 19, where the author argued that animals like owls, vultures, and apes possess sensory capabilities superior to humans. This section concludes that thought by establishing nature as the supreme teacher and warns that while nature provides true omens, deceptive spirits can sometimes manipulate these natural signs to mislead human observers.</meta>

-># THEOPHR[ASTUS] PARA[CELSUS]<-

...it is possible to believe that nature is the most powerful teacher and mistress. God is wonderful in all His creatures; for this reason, nature must be investigated to the utmost degree, for in these things she teaches many admirable presages <term>Presage: A sign or omen indicating a future event, often used here in a natural or semi-divine context.</term> regarding death, war, and so on, just as true philosophy <note>original: "vera philpsophia"</note> holds in other places. Even spirits do not refrain from mingling in these matters; however, they falsify omens <term>Augury: The practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of birds.</term> in the senses of animals and stir them up beyond nature as if they were natural. For example, when spirits appear to crows in the form of a corpse, they urge the birds to produce the loudest croakings. When the diviner hears these, he thinks they are natural and puts forward the most false judgment based on them; from this follows a contempt for nature in birds, and consequently, a contempt for God. The same thing happens with other creatures. 

It must be observed that every single region has its own animals with which it has been endowed by God. 

<margin>
Every animal provides presages for its own region.
</margin>

When something new is at hand within nature itself, the animal indicates this very thing, each according to the conditions of its own nature and as much as is appropriate for that region. For this reason, everyone should observe and consult with the greatest diligence the behaviors of the animals of their region and the daily changes of such habits...

<summary>Paracelsus argues that nature is the ultimate teacher of divine omens, though he warns that deceptive spirits can manipulate animal behavior to create false signs. He emphasizes that each geographic region has specific animals gifted by God to signal local natural changes.</summary>
<keywords>nature as teacher, divine omens, augury, deceptive spirits, regional animals, Paracelsus, crows as omens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the thought from page 20 regarding the necessity of consulting experts to interpret natural signs. It expands on the specific sensory capabilities of different animals and warns of the deceptive spirits that attempt to confuse human understanding of these omens.</meta>

# ON PRESAGES #

...men skilled in this matter, who can distinguish true presages <term>Presage: A natural sign or omen that predicts a future event.</term> from false ones. For no animal is found that lacks its own presage; rather, each one produces its own—now in this way, at other times in that. However, just as spirits strive to falsify all presages, they do the same with auguries <term>Augury: The practice of interpreting omens from the behavior and flight of birds.</term>. They attempt to twist what is appropriate for one animal—such as a peacock or a sparrow—and apply it to a dog or a cat. This causes people to look for signs in animals that do not even possess them: seeking in magpies what belongs to crows, or in sparrows what belongs to magpies. Thus, the greatest errors occur in these matters, just as they do in others. 

Likewise, horses have such sharp eyes that they see spirits and incantations <note>original: "incantationes"; here referring to magical spells or localized spiritual phenomena</note> and so on, by which they are terrified; they possess this sensitivity in their sense of smell as well as in their sight. Dogs also see spirits just as horses do, but they do not smell them. The same is true of hens, but especially the rooster, who hears everything—even the quietest things—while he is sleeping, though he does not smell them. 

Truly, the devil persecutes the beasts used by men just as much as he persecutes the men themselves, so that he may inflict harm upon the human race. When birds fly toward or away at an unusual time, or contrary to their natural habit, it is a presage. Also, when wild and rare birds fly in against their custom, where previously they were not seen...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, suggesting a continuation of the specific types of unusual bird behaviors that signal coming changes or dangers.</note>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that while every animal provides natural omens, deceptive spirits often "scramble" these signs to mislead humans. He details the specific supernatural sensitivities of horses, dogs, and roosters, noting that the devil targets domestic animals to indirectly harm humanity.</summary>

### THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS ###

...seen or heard, bring a presage. In all these matters, one must observe with the greatest care so that the devil does not deceive man. An abundance and plenty of fish is a sign of sudden mortality <term>Mortality: Here used to refer to a widespread outbreak of disease or a plague.</term>, for that 

<margin>Fertility is a presage of the plague.</margin>

usually takes its beginning from the abundance and fertility of the earth, just as summer drives the flowers from the trees in great profusion. Once everything has been driven out, the influence <term>Influence: In Paracelsian philosophy, this refers to invisible celestial or environmental forces that act upon the physical world and human health.</term> no longer has any enemies strong enough to resist it. Consequently, it rules according to its own will, for in that situation no mediating power <note>original: "media virtus"; referring to a balancing force or middle ground of health</note> remains between the healthy and the sick regarding this influence. One side is forced to yield while the other conquers. That which succumbs puts forth everything good within itself—much like when someone imagines their enemy, such as the plague, and while departing from their home, carries it away with them, etc. Similarly, when Nature foresees mortality, she displays a fruitfulness <note>original: "frugalitatem"; here used in the sense of a high yield of crops or "fruits of the earth" rather than thrift</note> beyond the region's custom; and after every vital strength has been cast out, bodies remain empty, so that nothing is left in them to offer resistance. Then, finally, death breaks in without opposition. Therefore, large and numerous fish and fertile years presage some future evil; not otherwise than...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that an unusual abundance of fish or crops is actually a warning of an approaching plague. He argues that Nature exhausts its protective strength through this over-fertility, leaving the environment and human bodies defenseless against lethal "influences."</summary>

# CONCERNING PRESAGES #

...of rivers, excesses and superfluous heat, cold and winds, as well as floods and similar occurrences which have rarely or never been seen before, indicate miseries to us: high prices for grain <note>original: "caritates annonarum"; referring to the high cost of food caused by scarcity, often synonymous with famine</note>, wars, plagues, and so on. For natural things overflow too much with their own moisture, just as when a person works or labors beyond their strength, so that being weak and feeble they are forced to lie down and rest for several days; for such a person cannot resist an enemy. We ought, therefore, to value such natural signs very highly, and understand them—not because it is [merely] necessary to know them, but as a warning which they prefigure, and for the recognition of other things which are necessary to know in natural philosophy <term>Natural Philosophy: The study of nature and the physical universe, which was the precursor to modern science.</term>.

<margin>Excess brings an evil presage.</margin>

The presages of divinations <note>original: "divinationum"; here referring to the practice of seeking knowledge of the future through non-natural or speculative means</note> are uncertain because they may happen or not, or occur slowly or quickly, etc. These are used especially by those who convince themselves that they possess the most wisdom above all others, as if they knew everything, and they are regarded as such by others. For example, if a physician <note>original: "Medicus"</note> says: "This sick man will not live much longer," yet the patient survives for a longer time than predicted and dies only later: the physician will still be able to claim he predicted it, even if the timing <note>original: "terminus"; the boundary or limit of time</note> deceived him. Divination indeed takes its origin from the [properties] of natural...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that extreme or unusual weather events serve as natural warnings of impending famine, war, and plague, comparing Nature's exhaustion to a laborer working past their limits. He also cautions against the uncertainty of speculative divination, using a physician's faulty prognosis as an example of how timing can be misread.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the uncertainty of divinations and predictions. The previous page ended by noting that a physician might claim to have predicted a death even if it occurs much later than expected; this page elaborates on that fallibility due to a lack of practical experience.</meta>

->THEOPHR. PARA<-

...and on the foundation of certain things, signs, and arts; yet it falls short in this respect, that it lacks sufficient experience. Nonetheless, those who use these methods believe they have divined with precision. For example, if someone—having learned of a certain rain through astronomy <note>original: "Astronomia"; in the 16th century, astronomy and astrology were deeply intertwined, and practitioners used celestial movements to predict weather and earthly events</note>—does not understand that the sun can prevent it, he nonetheless predicts it will happen. But when it rains a long time after the period he designated, he might say: "Behold, I spoke of this rain four weeks ago; I only wonder that it did not happen sooner." Likewise, a physician <note>original: "Medicus"</note> who observes that a patient's urine is black judges that the sick man will hardly live for three days. On the contrary, this blackness might actually provide the occasion for his life to be prolonged. This judgment then remains suspended in the air <note>A figurative expression meaning the prediction remains unfulfilled and "hanging" without a result</note> until the man eventually dies, which happens three years later. Then the physician says: "I predicted his death a long time ago, though I am surprised it was delayed for so long."

<margin>Divination from auguries <note>original: "Diuinatio ex auguriis"; the ancient practice of interpreting omens based on the behavior, flight, or sounds of birds</note>.</margin>

Some divinations also take their origin from auguries. For instance, if someone says, "This bird is behaving in a strange manner; it brings some news," but he predicts nothing definite <note>original: "nihil definitè prognoſticat"</note>. After one thing or another happens, he quickly says: "It seemed very clear to me that something new was going to happen," and so on. From sortileges <term>Sortilege: The practice of foretelling the future by drawing lots or casting objects, such as dice, stones, or marked papers.</term>

<summary>Paracelsus critiques the lack of practical experience in divinatory arts and medicine, mocking how practitioners retroactively claim accuracy for failed or delayed predictions. He uses examples from astronomy, uroscopy, and the interpretation of bird behavior to illustrate these logical fallacies.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, divination, astronomy, medical prognosis, augury, sortilege, experience vs theory, uroscopy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of fallible divination, specifically addressing the difference between "mere opinion" and the "true art" of reading nature's signs. It follows the previous page's discussion on how practitioners retroactively justify their predictions.</meta>

### ON PRESAGES
### (FOREKNOWLEDGE)

<margin>Divination from the casting of lots.</margin>
Likewise, as when something is perceived from <term>Geomancy</term> <note>original: "Geomantia"; a method of divination that interprets patterns formed by earth, stones, or sand.</note>, whatever it may be, if anything happens afterward, they claim that very event was what they had seen. It happens no differently for those who publish annual <term>prognostications</term> <note>Yearly astrological or weather predictions, common in the 16th century.</note>; if they have written anything concerning war, and any kind of conflict occurs afterward—even between two old women—they believe they have divined it. The same is true regarding peace. Even if one devil strikes another, the spirits did not consider that a war. So too with lightning, thunderbolts, storms, and so forth; for at one time or another, one of these things is bound to happen. For this reason, their divination is not a true science, but mere opinion. 

However, an opinion derived from external objects is good, such as those derived from natural things; this is a true and certain art. For a man does not possess this knowledge from within himself, but from nature, which produces signs from within itself and demonstrates its own character. This is the praise of nature, not of man. Likewise, a prophet is praised not when it is said, "this is a true prophet," but rather He from whom the prophet receives his word is praised—namely, God. Such people are accepted by God, and through them He speaks many things. Thus, natural <term>presage</term> <note>A sign or warning that a momentous event is about to happen; a natural omen.</note> also arises from nature, and afterward it becomes known to us through experience, just as the season is known from the flowers...

<vocab>Geomancy, prognostication, divination, spirit, prophet, presage, nature</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus argues that most divination is based on vague guesses and retroactive "proofs," whereas true insight comes from God and the "Light of Nature," which provides consistent, observable signs.</summary>
<keywords>divination, geomancy, nature, prophecy, theology of science, prognostications, natural signs</keywords>

# THEOPHR[ASTUS] PARA[CELSUS]

...summer. This knowledge does not come from the general notion where people simply say, "winter is now here, and later summer will come." For from that general idea, one does not yet know how quickly or how slowly it will arrive, or on exactly which day of the month. However, I know this exactly from the flowers. Likewise, when an eclipse of the Sun occurs, we know by experience that a calamity <margin>*Presage from an Eclipse.*</margin> is coming. Similarly, when other similar movements occur in the heavens, such as the <term>flowing of stars</term> <note>original: "stellarum fluxiones"; likely referring to meteors or shooting stars, which Paracelsus viewed as physical "excretions" or fruits of the celestial bodies.</note>: we recognize that stars in the heavens are like trees on the earth, which fall to the ground as they bear their fruits. It is no different with rain, snow, thunder, and the "fruits of the stars," which are born from their own branches and likewise <margin>*Fruits of the stars*</margin> fall to the earth. 

When, therefore, the <term>celestial garden</term> <note>A central Paracelsian concept where the cosmos is viewed as a living, organic garden; the stars are the "plants" and weather or terrestrial events are their "fruits."</note> becomes known to us, and we see what trees are planted within it, we recognize their fruits. From these fruits, through the experience of them, the trees themselves are identified. For an unlearned man learns to recognize a pear tree from the pear fruit, not the other way around. Afterward, through the tree and its branches, we learn over time what its autumn will be like, and its "year" in the heavens. Through the experience of such things, we can offer presages, and do so truly. For experience has produced this art over a long passage of time, and it belongs to this...

<meta>catchword: lila (likely a transcription of "illa")</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that true prophecy is based on the "experience" of nature's cycles, comparing stars to trees and weather events to their fruit. He argues that just as we know a tree by its fruit, we can predict celestial and earthly events by observing the "celestial garden."</summary>

### ON PRESAGES

...it is the first cornerstone and foundation upon which [knowledge] is built. For if someone had never seen snow, how would they know what color it was? Therefore, knowledge **takes its origin from the eyes**, <note>original: "ex oculis igitur sumit originem scientia"; Paracelsus is championing "autopsia" or seeing for oneself over relying solely on old books.</note> for they teach us constantly and are the masters of our understanding; without them, nothing can be completed. A peasant, through the experience of his eyes and the teachings of his predecessors, knows the natural conditions of his field and orchard. Likewise, through the experience of both of these—my own as well as that of the ancients—the heavens are explained, and so on. In short, apart from experience, no presage exists or carries any weight.

Similarly in medicine, every disease is a **fruit leading toward death**. That is to say, just as a tree that is dry in winter brings forth its buds when springtime rises, <margin>The buds of diseases are like those of trees</margin> then its leaves, then flowers, and finally fruit: in no other way does every disease first lie hidden within a person. It is "dry"—that is, in its own "winter," the disease does not yet appear. But when death desires to break out, it displays its signs one after another, just as trees do. <margin>The physician's experience.</margin> For this reason, a physician ought to recognize all diseases one after another, judging by the signs of experience that this person will fall into such an infirmity, and that person into another. # THEOPHR[ASTUS] PARA[CELSUS]

...**health** it will be able to act, since it too has its own signs, its progress, its own summer, its buds, leaves, flowers, and so on. There is, therefore, no other true use of **presages**, <note>original: "præsagiorum"; here meaning the ability to predict the course of a disease or event based on current signs.</note> nor any art more true, than that which is drawn from nature, rather than from **sorcery** or **auguries**. For any presage must arise from the specific nature of its own subject and possess its own field of knowledge. In this way, certain presages can be made—specifically, when each one is drawn from its own natural condition, in which it truly exists and reveals itself. 

For this reason, no one is forced to take a long and wandering path when they have the most direct and least deceptive way right at hand. For example, if one can recognize a disease in **urine**, <term>urine</term> <note>original: "lotio"; uroscopy, the study of urine, was a standard medical practice, though Paracelsus argues here for its use as a natural sign versus magical divination.</note> why is there any need to seek it in sorceries, crystals, beryl stones, <term>noboch</term>, <note>Likely a reference to a specific talisman or a term from the "magical" vocabulary of the era which Paracelsus is dismissing here.</note> and such, as soothsayers are accustomed to do? By doing this, you turn away from the truth to consort with **lying spirits**. In the end, you will not dare to affirm or propose anything true or certain from such sources, even though you are undoubtedly capable of doing so through nature.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural signs from page 27. # ON FOREKNOWLEDGE
## C 2

it is not a certain science, for those having black hair <note>original: "pilos"; referring to the previous discussion on hair color as a sign of temperament.</note> also do the same. Therefore, another science is held for this purpose, namely <term>Chiromancy</term> <note>From the Greek "cheir" (hand) and "manteia" (divination). Paracelsus treats this not as magic, but as a way to read the "Signatures" of nature written on the body.</note>, which encompasses within itself the wisdom, aptitude, and nature of man, etc. For whatever a person performs, they do with the observation of order and adornment; and nature does no differently, whether with a tree or anything else, each according to its own form, etc. So also nature works in the formation of a human being, according to its divisions, just as in forming a tree, a herb, and so on.

Therefore, just as one tree is distinguished from another tree by its own signs, in no other way is one person known from another except by the signs adhering to them. And just as it is said of trees, "this one is sweet, raw, or mild" in its fruits, etc.; so also is this man treacherous, envious, or cruel, while that one is mild and incontinent <note>original: "incontinens"; meaning lacking self-control or unrestrained.</note>, and so forth.

Wherefore, in Chiromancy, there must be the experience of this knowledge—not that if someone had a hooked line in their hand and was hanged, another person having a similar line in their hand must necessarily undergo the same death. For that person is not placed in that nature, but from spirits, etc. <note>Paracelsus is arguing against a fatalistic or "fortune-telling" view of palmistry, suggesting that lines indicate character and potential rather than a fixed, unavoidable destiny.</note> which in this place <meta>catchword: mini</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus argues that chiromancy is a legitimate natural science because it reads the "signs" of a person's inner nature, similar to how one identifies a tree's qualities by its exterior. He cautions that these signs indicate a person's natural disposition rather than a predestined fate.</summary>

<keywords>chiromancy, palmistry, nature of man, natural signs, signatures, character, formation of man</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 29, where Paracelsus argued that Chiromancy is not a matter of superstitious fortune-telling but a way of understanding a person's inherent nature through the "signatures" or forms found in the body.</meta>

<header>THEOPHR. PARA.</header>

<margin>Chiromancy.</margin>
...is not at all fitting. But indeed, if I find a line corresponding to a fig tree within that same <term>anatomy</term> <note>original: "anatomia"; Paracelsus uses this term to refer to the structural correspondence between a human body part and a part of nature, rather than a physical dissection.</note>, I will then be able to say that the nature of that man is to be compared to the said tree. That is to say, the man is weak by nature, inconsistent, fickle, and so on—because in his hand, a tree or herb of such a figure or form has been compared. Unless this is done, no certain or true art will be present here; that is, unless the lines of the hands are compared to trees. When that comparison has been made, it must be said: this man is raw, inept, and unrefined in his morals and reason.

Therefore, when any hand in its anatomy is found to be like a peach tree, say: this man is quickly angered, but his anger is also quickly calmed. Likewise, he is not of a pure heart; his every word has thorns, meaning they bring with them an indigestible poison, just as in the peach fruit <note>original: "malo persico"; literally "Persian apple," the common Latin term for a peach. Paracelsus is likely comparing the peach's fuzzy exterior or its hard, bitter pit to the man's personality.</note>; the stone <note>original: "os"; literally "bone," referring here to the hard pit or "stone" of the peach.</note> cannot be easily broken, and so it is with other similar things.

<margin>Physiognomy.</margin>
In no different way does <term>Physiognomy</term> <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note> have its own art, just like a mineral mine. There are various signs by which minerals can be recognized, no one being similar to another. Sometimes a "bad" sign is even useful for correcting another bad sign, so that it may be certain. Wherefore...

<meta>catchword: pter (likely "propter," meaning "on account of")</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus explains that Chiromancy and Physiognomy are scientific systems of comparison, where lines in the hand are read as "signatures" of trees or minerals to reveal a person's inner character. He uses the fig tree as a sign of inconsistency and the peach tree as a sign of quick temper and a "hard" heart.</summary>

<header>ON SIGNS.</header>
<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: certainly</meta>

...more things are required in these matters. Thus, regarding the faces of men, a person might be upright and live honorably, yet still bear a sign indicating something bad, as was said above concerning minerals. In a human, the face is a mixture; it is a certain presage of all the properties existing within him, whatever they may be. Just as all the qualities of minerals—dross, <term>realgar</term> <note>original: "realgar"; a bright red arsenic sulfide mineral, often used in alchemy to represent volatile or toxic qualities.</note>, gold, silver, and so on—are cast into the fire, and through testing, that which is solid within them remains; so too must one consider all the signs in *Physiognomy* <note>The art of judging character from facial features.</note> together. These signs are proven in times of great necessity, and thus hidden properties are discovered.

For a person can present himself outwardly as upright, constant, mild, and truthful, even if he possesses evil and treacherous physiognomic signs; however, he hides and represses the fruits of those signs through careful effort. If you wish to examine him, treat him like silver and put him to the test: ask to borrow a sum of money from him, which you know he possesses or is able to lend. Now you have the fire by which you may easily recognize what vice or deception lies hidden within him. Likewise, if you think someone is constant, and you wish to be certain, examine him when necessity...

<summary>Paracelsus compares the human face to a mineral ore, arguing that true character is often hidden by social effort but can be revealed through the "fire" of stressful tests or necessity. He uses the example of a loan request as a practical way to expose a person's hidden vices or virtues.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, character testing, alchemy, minerals, realgar, ethics, signatures, gold and silver assaying</keywords>

->THEOPHR. PARA.<-
<meta>catchword: men</meta>

...demands [it], or feign a necessity; if he is someone other than you supposed, he will fly off from the fire like <term>realgar</term> <note>original: "realgar"; a volatile arsenic mineral used here to describe someone whose true, perhaps toxic, nature is revealed under heat or pressure.</note>. Therefore, every one of these signs must be diligently noted, as well as how nature herself marks humans in their formations. For if the <term>anatomy</term> <note>In Paracelsian terms, "anatomy" refers to the inner essential structure or the "blueprint" of a thing rather than just a physical dissection.</note> of a pear tree is taken up, a pear tree will come from it; if indeed it is of gold, a golden man will be made, and so forth. In this manner, nature fashions for each person their own conditions, so that one becomes like a pear tree, another like a fig tree, another like iron, and so on. This is not because nature compels men to this—even though she produces many types, properties, and so on—for none of these should have dominion over us, but only God. It is fitting for us to be humans, not <term>beasts</term> <note>original: "bruta"; referring to irrational animals lacking a soul or higher reason.</note>; yet if we wish to choose the life of beasts, nature also wills that we be recognized as beast-like.

<margin>Baptism restrains the beast within man.</margin>
However, Baptism takes away all beast-like conditions from us. Therefore, nature signs nothing in a human except that which was beast-like in him, or in his animal nature. In this way, nature imprints certain signs upon thieves and robbers, and consequently, certain and true <term>presages</term> <note>Forewarnings or omens of future behavior.</note> can be made regarding them, insofar as they act naturally; but as was said above, nature is often broken. Nature [makes] blind men...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that while nature imprints humans with specific characters—likening them to trees or minerals—these natural inclinations do not force human behavior because God and Baptism can overcome "beastly" instincts.</summary>

...nature produces men from birth, yet not without cause; such marked men are not found among the order of Christ. <term>Philosophy</term> <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to natural philosophy—the study of the laws of nature and the physical world.</note> can recognize that the lame—excluding those ordained for the glory of God <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 9:3, where Jesus explains that a man was born blind not due to sin, but so the works of God could be revealed through him.</note>—are produced by nature in this way for the reason that they would otherwise have turned out too <margin>Marked by nature.</margin> wicked. Nature, through her foreknowledge, made them such so that they would be unable to carry out their [evil] endeavors; and in this same way, the mute are born, and so on. Indeed, nature does nothing in vain. Since man, who is her work, observes this very thing, how much more does her craftsman know and understand it? For he possesses a greater intellect; and according to the intention he has for a man, he impresses a sign upon him. For Christ speaks so that the works of God may be known; likewise, the lame are not born without cause. Nature considers these species, and for their formation she takes up what is fitting: silver for silver cups, iron for a horseshoe, and so forth. She produces each thing in its own form, inducing one shape for the cup and another for the tool. In this way, nature fashions wondrous children on the earth. ...[poisonous] things are not to be recounted in this place, for all regions are full of them, not only on painted linens and tablets, and so forth, but also in signs in the heavens and on the earth, along with the times of events happening to us daily. Therefore, so that these matters—more than marvelous as they are—may be known, everyone will be able to look into the play <note>original: *ludum*; likely referring to the "theatrical display" or interplay of prophetic symbols and events.</note> of another through these charts or figures. In short: it is a common madness that rages until it is fulfilled.

And the Prophets have their explanation in the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, in Paul, and in the other Apostles; and so that all these things may be truly understood, they must be explained through the grace of God by those to whom He has granted it.

-> *Of Meteorological Impressions.* <-

### CHAPTER ONE.

We have spoken previously regarding meteors <note>In the 16th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena—such as rain, wind, rainbows, or lightning—not just objects falling from space.</note>, stating in the preface that all meteorological operations exist within the three primes <note>original: *tribus primis*; Paracelsus’s three fundamental chemical principles: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which he believed composed all matter.</note>, and are also brought about through them. The firmament <note>The sphere of the heavens and the stars.</note> and the elements are within them, and from them arise the "impressions" <note>original: *impressiones*; celestial or atmospheric influences that leave a physical or spiritual mark on the world.</note> which it is by no means necessary to place in this third book of meteors, since they were discussed in the preceding books. From these [we] col... <note>original: *col...*; likely *colligimus* or "we collect/conclude," though the word is interrupted by the page break.</note>
